RAGA WISE SONGS IN HINDI AND MARATHI PLAIN LIST WITHOUT URLs OF SONGS

**RAGA WISE SONGS IN HINDI AND MARATHI**

**PLAIN LIST *WITHOUT* URLs OF SONGS**

**REVISED AND ENLARGED FINAL VERSION: 5th APRIL 2026**

**Shrikant G Talageri**

****

\[This article was originally posted on 23 November 2025 without classical pieces or the URLs of the songs. On 9 January 2026, I reuploaded the article with a *few* beautiful *short classical pieces* added to the lists

*Today, **5** **April** **2026**, I am uploading the **final** version of the article in two versions*:

1. **This** article containing the plain list, without URLs and instrumental pieces.

2. The same article with the youtube URLs of every song and classical piece, (including the same classical piece sung by different maestros) to illustrate and give the feel of the rāga, and, more pertinently, with URLs of every song so that the songs can be heard on this article itself by clicking on the respective URLs. It will also have examples of instrumental pieces in every rāga. This second, and much longer, article will be here:

<https://talageri.blogspot.com/2026/01/blog-post.html>



*The articles are dedicated to my father **Gangadhar** **Sitaram** **Talageri**, who would have completed exactly **100** **years** **of** **age** today, and to my mother **Shaila** **Gangadhar** **Talageri** nee **Sita** **Pandurang** **Taggarse**.* Both my parents were passionate lovers of music: my mother a dedicated singer, and my father the possessor, lover and regular listener of a huge collection of gramophone records (and later cassettes) which instilled in me appreciation for classical music from my school days. I have tried to search out (on youtube) and include in this collection all the short classical pieces I remember hearing on his gramophone records and cassettes\].****

A few preliminary points:

*I am not myself able to recognize rāgas by ear. I have always regretted not having formally studied classical music, but never so poignantly or bitterly as when preparing these lists*. Hence the lists in this article are not based on my own expertise. It is only my effort to try to present as complete lists as possible for the *general* *reference* of music lovers. *Needless to say, I have consulted various different sources and tried to present composite lists. In the process there may be various errors in assignation of rāgas (in respect of the Hindi and Marathi songs and even the natyageets) to particular songs, so these must be treated as preliminary lists and any particular song cross-checked.* I am assuming errors in name of singer etc. will be minimal if any. If anyone notices any errors, I will be grateful to have them pointed out so I can correct them.

But first, a very necessary long digression on the very concept of **rāgas**:

Is the concept of **rāga** itself valid? From my experiences while preparing this article, I would not be surprised at some cynic claiming that the whole concept of **rāga** is a hoax: there are just tunes and tunes, and the *same* tune can be called by *different* “**rāga**” names, and *different* tunes can be called by the *same* “**rāga**” name. The tune, as used in any song, can be beautiful regardless of what “**rāga**” name is given to it. So just enjoy the music! \[Indeed, the exteme contempt with which musicians and music-lovers (see below) treat the concept of “**rāga**” names would seem to justify this cynical viewpoint\].

But I strongly disagree: there *is* such a thing as “**rāga**”, and knowing the **rāga** name can add both intellectually *and* aesthetically to the enjoyment of any song. And the general contemptuous abuse of “**rāga**” names does not justify this cynical attitude. In classical music, especially as used in film songs (or even in semi-classical pieces), there is general laxity in strict application of the rules of which notes *should* be used, and which should *not*, in a particular rāga. This is not necessarily a bad thing if it enhances the beauty of the song: after all a song is supposed to please the ears, soothe the mind, or tug at the heartstrings, and not to follow rigorous dry rules. But as a result, it is often difficult to guess or decide the **rāga** if it seems based on a mixture of two or more **rāgas**. But any “**rāga**” based song (unless conscious attempts are made to mix together two or more different **rāgas** – but then in that case a new or different “**rāga**” name should be given) *does* belong to a particular **rāga**, even when the notes and rules are tweaked. *What is truly inexcusable (as anyone will find out on searching through the data) is the tendency to arbitrarily (and without detailed explanation) give *any* “**rāga**” name to *any* song*.

I have a massive collection of **Hindi** and **Marathi** songs on my computer. But when I sat down to search out on the internet (from the above) the **rāga** of any song which was not already on my list of “**rāga-wise songs**” (or which for some reason I felt necessary to recheck), I ended up with chaotic results. Not to mention the backache, eye-strain and dizziness resulting from endlessly sitting in front of a computer scouring the internet.

The case of “**Artificial** **Intelligence**” sources is even more atrocious. Ask on google a question about any song **ABC**, “**ABC is in which raag**” and the “**AI** **overview**” will reply “**ABC is in raag X**”. After that, ask “**Is ABC in raag X**”, and it will more often than not reply either “**ABC is not in raag X**” or “**ABC is in raag Y**”. *The **Google** “**AI overview**” almost invariably and compulsively gives a different **rāga** for any song from the one given in the **Google** “**AI** **Mode**”, and very often *both* sharply contradict their *own* earlier results.* It would have been funny if it were not so frustrating and infuriating.

Of the four categories of songs included in my lists, the *worst* case is in respect of **Marathi** songs (film, bhavgeets, abhangs, etc.). If you search on google, *usually no **rāga** is mentioned anywhere for the massively overwhelming majority of songs*. And where any **rāga** *is* mentioned, there is the usual plethora of *many* *different* **rāgas** confidently asserted by different sources for the same song.

The case is only slightly better in the case of **Hindi** (film and non-film) songs. I tried to check *many* different sources whenever some song is assigned to *different* **rāgas** in different places: more or less a guesswork or consensus process (while comparing with already known songs from the same **rāga**. ***But it is truly frustrating to see the same song featuring in the lists of totally different rāgas. It is necessary for an official group of experts (in rāga knowledge and/or in recognizing rāgas by ear) to sit down and examine all the songs, note for note, and classify them correctly. Sadly, I have had to brutally exclude literally *countless* beautiful rāga-based songs from my list because of the chaotic variation in the rāgas assigned to them in different places, making even a broad-consensus guesstimate difficult***.

In the case of **Marathi** **natyageet**, which are clearly based on **rāgas**, one would expect more clarity. But no! In their case, these being at least proudly semi-classical (if not fully classical), one would have expected some importance being given to the **rāga**. But, *it appears that the concept of **rāga** is held in such contempt by singers and video uploaders and so-called lovers of natyageets or even good music, that if one checks out a majority of natyageets (let alone other more popular varieties of songs not expected to be strictly classical) on google or even on youtube, the one thing about the songs which the uploaders (of the information or video) seem determined to keep a deep dark secret is the actual name of the **rāga** (except, of course, in those numerous cases where we find contributors scrambling over each other to give a plethora of names of different **rāgas** for the same song on different sites)! All kinds of unnecessary data, except the name of the relevant **rāga**, is freely proferred and available*! To give a most telling example, the most famous **nāndī** (group opening song) from **Marathi** **sangeet**-**nataks** (“**Panchatunda Nararunda Maladhar**” from **Sangeet** **Shakuntal**) has various sources (including **AI** ones) giving variously **Bilawal**, **Khamaj**, **Miya-Malhar**, **Jaijaivanti** and even **Hamsadhvani** or one or two other **rāgas**! Needless to say, all the “expert” music-lovers singing and uploading versions of the song on Youtube, or pontificating on the song in articles on the internet, seem to think the name of the **rāga** in which it is composed has zero value! Likewise I had to delete the beautiful **natyageet** **Yadumani** **Sadanaa** (and countless **Hindi** songs like **Kal** **Raat** **Zindagi** **Se** **Mulaqat** **Ho** **Gayi**) from my earlier list because I found it had multiple attributions: though finally I have now included it under the ambiguous name Jangla. \[Finally, one lady pointed out to me that the tune of “**Panchatunda Nararunda Maladhar**” was exactly that of “**Vaishnav Jan** **To**” by Lata Mangeshkar (the film version by Mukesh is in a different **rāga** **Kedar**), so I could pinpoint the **rāga** as **Khamaj**\].

At the same time all the sources (i.e. whichever do profess any view) say **Le Ja Uski Duwaen** from **Heer** and **Dil Leke Daga Denge Wo** from **Naya Daur** (both sung by Mohd Rafi, and both seeming to have exactly the same tune) are in two different **rāgas**, **Bhairavi** and **Piloo** respectively! Likewise the three similar-sounding songs (all three by Mukesh) **Naina Hai Jadoo Bhare** from **Bedard Zamana Kya Jane**, **Gaye Ja Geet Milan Ke** from **Mela**, and **Chhotisi Ye Zindagani Re** from **Aah**, are given many chaotically different names; and a person supposed to be an expert on **rāgas**, when consulted, escapistically replied that they were all in “**mishra**” **rāgas** – there is no such thing as a bald “**mishra**” **rāga**, though there can be “**mishra** **Kafi**”, “**mishra** **Khamaj**” etc., i.e. versions of *particular* ***rāgas*** called “**mishra**” because they freely use notes and features not really instrinsic to that **rāga** - **while still remaining within that **rāga****!

Naturally I had to exclude all these songs from my lists.



And while the case should be (expected to be) distinctly better in the case of **semi**-**classical** **pieces** sung by old eminent singers and made into gramophone records, that is a forlorn hope: even the gramophone records and cassettes of countless clearly **semi**-**classical** forms like **thumris**, etc. do not mention the **rāga**. To give two glaring examples:

a) The famous song **hamaar** **kahi** **mano** **rajaji** sung by **BegumAkhtar** (and occasionally by others, as well as in a filmi version) has been put up on **youtube** umpteen times by umpteen people without mentioning the **rāga** (simply calling it ***thumri*** or ***dadra*** as if that is a ***rāga***, or as if ***thumris*** and ***dadras*** are not supposed to be in ***rāgas***). A *few* do mention the **rāga**: variously **Piloo**, **Mishra** **Maand**, **Bhairavi**, **Kafi**, **Kiravani**, and **Khamaj**!\!

b) Similarly, the most famous song of BadeGhulamAliKhan, **aye** **na** **baalam** **ka** **karun** **sajani**, with a filmi version sung by Yesudas, is firmly categorized as **Sindhu** **Bhairavi** in many places, **Piloo** in many others, **Kiravani** in yet others, even **Jogiya** in a few, or usually simply called “**thumri**”!!

Even more amazingly, while the uploaders of classical pieces give endless irrelevant details, and long stories, about their uploads, *most of they seem to have a psychopathic mental block when it comes to giving the words (i.e. the title-words) of the piece*. If words are so irrelevant and one must just enjoy the music, why bother to give the names of even the singer and the rāga: why not just give a silly nondescript title such as “a nice song from India” in the description of the video, and leave everything else to the imagination of the listener?

So if the title-words given by me (based on utter frustration after desperate failed attempts to catch the words by ear) are wrong for any song, I am not responsible for it. *The three most essential points about a classical upload which should be mentioned are the names of the singer, the rāga and the title-words of the song. A fourth essential would be the tāl*. Only a person having deep intrinsic contempt for classical music would deliberately upload a classical piece without giving these details: *though there are of course some sincere uploaders who confess that some of these particular details are unknown to them and that they are uploading the pieces only for the musical enjoyment of the listeners*. I had to exclude countless beautiful classical pieces from the list because the words, after repeated hearings, were undecipherable, and the videos themselves failed to provide the words.

\[It is this common “classical” tendency to harbour utter contempt for words which leads to classical music being parodied, e.g. in films, etc., as consisting of mumbled or slurred gobbledygook or nonsense-words. Incidentally, after many hours of frustrating attempts to catch the words of **Amir** **Khan’s** **Pancham** **Malkauns**, or to search them out on the internet, I decided to just omit it from the list, like many other such pieces!\]

A noteworthy and refreshing exception are the uploads of **Manali** **Bose**, where we get not only the **rāga** names but also the lyrics.

\[I think **rāgdārī** in Indian music can only be saved if **Vishnu** takes on a special **avatār** and comes down to earth with the single-minded purpose of saving it, especially from our singers, artistes, musicologists, “experts” and youtube uploaders!\]

And lately, as I continued my (re)search in Google and youtube, I started (*repeatedly*) receiving a message from Google saying that they were detecting “**unusual traffic**” on my computer and asking me to click on a “**reCAPTCHA checkbox**” labelled “**I’m not a robot**”!

*So ultimately I am basically uploading an incomplete and insufficient list which could do with expert checking*, since *pānī gale tak ā gayā hai*, and I can take it no more. Or else the article will never be uploaded. \[Yes, I have no option but to brazenly “pass the buck” for any wrong **rāga** ascriptions!\]

*So of course, after all my enforced cross-checking and nitpicking, there may still be many more songs in the lists below which could call for a reappraisal of the allotted rāga: so this article must be treated as a preliminary list to be corrected, enlarged and improved by genuine music experts with better musical recognition than myself*.

The silver lining is that music is to be enjoyed, and a song or piece of music does not become less enjoyable to the listener if he/she (primarily because of the indolent indifference of “musical experts”) does not know for sure the correct name of the rāga or the exact words, and we will have to satisfy ourselves with this thought.

**

***\[NOTE***: Some rāgas are so similar, or so deceptively “different” that people regularly identify the same songs in two different rāgas. I have therefore provided combined lists of such almost-twin-clone rāgas: **Bhoop** and **Deskar**, **Des** and **Tilak** **Kamod**, **Asavari** and **Jaunpuri**/**Jeevanpuri**. Likewise I am clubbing together **Marwa** and **Pooriya**. *All this is for the purpose of this article only*. Likewise though **Todi** and **Gujari** **Todi** are distinguished by the presence of **Pa** in the former and its absence in the latter, I have combined the lists because of different assignation in different sources. Therefore cross-checking will become necessary in all these cases.

Likewise, because of the apparently insoluble ambiguity of the **rāga** of “**Yaad Piya Ki Aye**”, I have combined **rāgas** **Bhinnashadja** and **Hemant** in one. Also **Megh**, though apparently not exactly the same as **Megh** **Malhar**, has been included among **Malhar**.

But for *really* *great* and *deep* musical appreciation, see the following superb video, where the musicologist (**Dr. Vidhyadhar Oke**) seems to be playing a 22 ***shruti*** harmonium, and where he shows the difference ***in*** ***shrutis*** in the two rāgas (**Marwa** and **Pooriya**):

[https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JdmSKmG6A2A](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JdmSKmG6A2A)

Likewise, see also the following wonderful video which tells us that the difference between **Bhoop** and **Deskar** lies in the different ***śrutis*** used, and demonstrates this:

[https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=U1absCjaZFQ](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=U1absCjaZFQ)

But, as I said, being no expert myself in ***rāgas*** and notes by ear, I will still present, in this article, combined lists of songs in **Marwa** and **Pooriya**, in **Bhoop** and **Deskar**, etc.\]

******

Finally, one more disclaimer, and one claim:

**Disclaimer**: Due to the propensity of some Classical singers to blur the words, and of the record companies and youtube uploaders to avoid specifying the **rāga** and/or the exact wordings, there may possibly be some wrong guesswork on my part in the wordings of some of the short classical videos uploaded. The listener will have to use his/her own ears and guessing powers if he/she wants the exact and correct wordings.

**Claim**: By listening to and comparing the various videos (light as well as Classical) for each **rāga**, the attentive listener may hopefully become adept at recognizing **rāgas** by ear.

Also, as I have only put up Short Classical videos (I have generally avoided including classical pieces of very great length i.e., say, above 20 minutes, though a few slightly longer ones may inadvertently appear in the lists), *if* anyone has (or develops) an interest in any particular singer or longer versions of any **rāga** or particular classical song, the internet in general and **youtube** in particular (with this article as a base) are always there for further listening.

Finally, it is sad that the younger generation is moving sharply away from good music and this really makes one frightened to think of the future of music in general. *But then perhaps I am wrong*: even in/since my childhood days I often got this same feeling, and today I see so many people of my own age and even older whose musical preferences are as bad as what I said above about the “younger generation” that I hope I am wrong in my fears. I particularly remember an incident in my office ten years ago (2015-16): I was working in the Inward Clearing Dept. of Central Bank of India, in a hall with at least 70-90 co-workers. As we worked, some people put music on their mobiles or recorders. All kinds of nonsense (in my opinion) was often being put: I have referred to one such incident in my article on falsetto singing. In this particular case, one man was putting old songs. The song which came up was **Lata** **Mangeshkar’s** incomparable “**Ja** **Ja** **Re** **Ja** **Baalamwa**” from **Basant** **Bahar**. Suddenly, one person called out: “**arey ye kya faaltu gaane lagaate ho**!”, and to my amazement many voices (of people in their early fifties!) joined in the chorus of condemnation, and the poor man shut off the song. I was livid with outrage and fury, but, apart from being struck stunned and speechless, for some reasons that I don’t recall I was not in the mood to kick up a shindy at that moment, and then the moment passed. But it taught me that musical-moronism is present in large sections of all generations.

But at the same time, when one sees younger generation people who make you feel that there is plenty of hope, such fears for the future of good music seem unfounded. Just two out of possibly **countless** examples:


[https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gYiRRS1Qpu8](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=gYiRRS1Qpu8)

[https://www.youtube.com/@arnavclassical/shorts](https://www.youtube.com/@arnavclassical/shorts)

Of course, no-one can predict the future. Certainly during the preparation of this article, I at least derived undescribable musical pleasure (as distinct from the physical tortures of sitting hunched up over the computer, and getting backaches and neckaches and sprained arms and fingers, apart from eye-strain and dizziness). Ultimately there is nothing like music to lift the mood and drive away the blues.

******

**APPENDIX: MULTI-RAGA SONGS**

There are certain well-known and popular songs which showcase many rāgas within one song.

But first note the two following songs which (in order to promote National Integration) depict songs from different parts of India (the first song in those actual languages, and the second song in parody):

**Teen Batti Char Rasta**:

Bengali, Tamil, Sindhi, Marathi, Gujarati, Banaras-Hindi, Punjabi:

[https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8wrTQCoJHDc](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=8wrTQCoJHDc)

**Shabnam**:

Hindi, Bengali, Gujarati, Marathi, Tamil, Punjabi:

[https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=XmzI4T6kmVc](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=XmzI4T6kmVc)



There are also the “parody” songs which feature verses being parodies of tunes of earlier famous songs, and usually for “comedy” effect. One example, and perhaps one of the earliest of such songs (and not especially a “comedy” song, but a “children’s stage program” song) is from the film **Main** **Chup** **Rahungi**:

[https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rSrzdNbmKlQ](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=rSrzdNbmKlQ)

The song has parodies of the following earlier songs:

Aye Na Baalam Ka Karun Sajani (thumri).

Chahe Koi Mujhe Jangli Kahe.

Ma Pyari Ma.

Itna Na Mujhse Tu Pyar Badha.

Ehsaan Tera Hoga Mujhpar.

Bikhra Ke Zulfen Chaman Mein Na Jana.

Dadi Amma Dadi Amma Maan Jao.

Husnwale Tera Jawab Nahin.

Ik Raaat Mein Do Do Chand Khile.

Dhoondho Dhoondho Re Sajana.

Zara Samne To Aao Chhaliye.

Lekar Pehle Pehla Pyar.

*These “parody” songs are obviously not included in the above lists*.



There are various *short* *versions* of songs (in different rāgas) in the film joined together to show a series of musical programs being conducted by a singer. Usually the *entire* *songs* are available in audio form. A prime example is the song from **Mamta** by **Lata** **Mangeshkar** which contains three different songs in brief:

[https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=GqpuhA\_\_Igo](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=GqpuhA__Igo)

The songs and **rāgas** are:

Ham Gawanwa Na Jaibe Ho Bina Jhoolni. **Basant** **Mukhari**.

Sakal Ban Gagan Pawan Chalat Purvai Re. **Bahar**.

Vikal Mora Manwa Un Bin Haye. **Jogiya**.



In another dance song by **Lata**-**Manna** from the film **Phoolon** **Ki** **Sej** (the only other film with music by **Adi Narayan Rao**, after the much earlier **Suvarna** **Sundari**, see below), all the **rāgas** and verses are independent but integrated into one whole:

[https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=1fluFEu_Dsw](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=1fluFEu_Dsw)

The songs and **rāgas** are:

Aa Bhi Ja Rasiya. **Adana**.

Chhum Chhum Nachat Kanhai. **Miya-Malhar**.

Ja Re Ja Kanha Ja Re Ja. **Piloo**.

Hamse Na Rootho Radha. **Khamaj**.

Man Mohana Piya Tu Jo Mila. **Malkauns**.



Sometimes, one song is embedded within another in the film, but both are also available separately as audios, like the song from **Dekh** **Kabira** **Roya**:

[https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7SIWWFCA7xc](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=7SIWWFCA7xc)

The songs are:

Meri Veena Tum Bin Roye. **Ahir** **Bhairav**.

Tu Pyar Kare Ya Thukraye. **Bhairavi**.

These songs are (as far as available) given separately in the above lists.



And finally, we have the songs which use different **rāgas** in each verse. Usually the verse-endings come back to the main **rāga** of the song.

Just a few prominent examples:

**Kuhu Kuhu Bole Koyaliya (Suvarna Sundari) Lata Rafi**.

[https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ANfV8VnzrYs&list=RDANfV8VnzrYs&start_radio=1](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ANfV8VnzrYs&list=RDANfV8VnzrYs&start_radio=1)

Main line: **Sohani**.

Saja Singar Ritu Ayi Basanti. **Sohani**.

Kahe Ghata Mein Bijli Chamke. **Bahar**.

Chandrika Dekh Chhayi. **Jaunpuri**.

Saras Raat Man Bhaye Priyatama. **Yaman**.

**Saaz Ho Tum Awaz Hoon Main (Saaz Aur Awaz) Rafi**.

[https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e6szCRfApKE&list=RDe6szCRfApKE&start_radio=1](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=e6szCRfApKE&list=RDe6szCRfApKE&start_radio=1)

Main Line: **Yaman**.

Mere Geet Ko Geet Na Samjho Pyar Ki Hai Ye Sargam. **Patdeep**.

Prem Tarana Rang Pe Aya Roop Ne Li Angdai. **Madhuvanti**.

Tan Man Jhoome Gagan Ko Choome. **Yaman** + **Bhairavi**.



Many of these songs pertain to changing seasons with each verse composed in a **rāga** believed to be representing the particular season described in the particular verse, but some others simply use different **rāgas** in different verses for musical effect or are children’s songs or tell stories. Just a few examples of these multi-**rāga** songs:
